NO MEASURABLE will be defined as no detectable\nconcentration of TRC as determined by any approved method established in 40 CFR Part 136.\nThe effluent limitation for TRC is the instantaneous maximum and cannot be averaged for\nreporting purposes. The maximum dechlorinated TRC shall be monitored daily by grab sample.\nTRC shall be measured within fifteen (15) minutes of sampling.\n\fPERMIT NO. NM0020583                       FACT SHEET                              Page 12\n\n               v. Total Dissolved Solids\n\nThe Colorado River flows more than 1400 miles from it headwaters in the Rocky Mountains\nthrough portions of seven states and the Republic of Mexico before it discharges into the Gulf of\nCalifornia. The Colorado River Basin spans approximately 155 million acres of The United\nStates, providing drinking water to 39 million people in both the US and Mexico and\nirrigation water to 5.5 million acres. Salinity impacts have been a major concern in the United\nStates and Mexico. The salinity of the Colorado River increases as it flows downstream. The\nColorado River has carried an average salt load of approximately 9 million tons annually past\nHoover Dam, the uppermost location at which numeric criteria have been established. Many of\nthe saline sediments of the Basin were deposited in prehistoric marine environments. Salts\ncontained within the sedimentary rocks are easily eroded, dissolved, and transported into the\nriver system. (Source: 2014 Review, Water Quality Standards for Salinity, Colorado River\nSystem - coloradoriversalinity.org).\n\nIn 1973, the Colorado River Basin States came together in 1973 and organized the Colorado\nRiver Basin Salinity Control Forum (Forum). In 1974, in coordination with the Department of\nthe Interior and the U.S. State Department, the Forum worked with Congress in the passage of\nthe Colorado River Basin Salinity Control Act (Act). Since implementation of the Program,\nmeasures have been put in place which now reduce the annual salt load of the Colorado River by\nmore than 1.3 million tons. The salinity concentration at Imperial Dam has been reduced by\nabout 90 mg/L. (Source: Colorado River Basin Salinity Control Forum-coloradoriversalinity.org)\n\nAccording to the Forum-adopted NPDES permit program policies (for Municipal Discharges), in\norder for a permittee to be in compliance with the Forum’s municipal discharges criteria, the\nincremental increase in salinity shall be 400 mg/L or less, which is considered to be a reasonable\nincremental increase above the flow weighted average salinity of the intake water supply.\nHowever, the permitting authority may permit a discharge in excess of the 400 mg/L incremental\nincrease at the time of issuance or reissuance of a NPDES discharge permit, upon satisfactory\ndemonstration by the permittee that it is not practicable to attain the 400 mg/L limit.\n\nApril 10, 2015, the facility demonstrated that it was economically impractical to attain the net\n400 mg/L incremental increase limit at that time. Because of that demonstration, EPA allowed a\ntemporary 30-day average TDS net incremental increase limit of 497 mg/L for the previous\npermit cycle, provided that the facility will diligently control and reduce TDS in their discharge\n(i.e., maintaining the formulated BMP activities, monitoring its effluent quality and quantity on a\nregular basis, and completing $14 million upgrades at the WWTP to improve overall effluent\nquality (i.e., reducing TDS in their discharges)).\n\nThe City of Farmington has constructed a new sampling station where water intake samplings\nhave been conducted. EPA believes the TDS data collected during the past permit cycle are\nsufficient and representative of facility existing operation conditions and can be used to derive\nthe final 30-day average net incremental increase limit. EPA cannot grant the permittee request\nof keeping the temporary 30-day average TDS net incremental increase limit of 497 mg/L in the\ndraft permit. To protect and maintain existing water quality as required by 40 CFR §122(4)(d)\nand to prevent further degradation of water quality in the San Juan River in accordance with 40\nCFR §131.12, Subsection A of 20.6.4.8 NMAC, the New Mexico Continuing Planning Process –\n\fPERMIT NO. NM0020583                        FACT SHEET                               Page 13\nAnti-degradation Policy Implementation Procedures, and § 201 of Navajo Nation Clean Water\nAct – Anti-degradation Policy, EPA proposes a revision to the facility temporary 30-day average\nTDS net incremental increase limit of 497 mg/L to 449 mg/L. The proposed TDS permit limit,\nin accordance with 40 CFR §133.101(f), was based 95th percentile of the most recent three years\n(i.e., 2018-2020) of City’s wastewater treatment plant effluent net TDS data. The proposed\nlimits take effluent variability within control of the facility into account and, at same time, would\nnot cause further degradation of the receiving water and its downstream. This approach is\nconsistent with the one used in developing the temporary 30-day average TDS net incremental\nincrease limit of 497 mg/L in the previous permit. The 30-day average mass loading limit is,\nalso, revised to 24,992lbs/day from 27,664 lbs/day based on the proposed 30-day average TDS\nnet incremental increase limit of 449 mg/L. The facility is required to continue maintaining the\nformulated BMP activities and monitoring its effluent quality and quantity on a regular basis\nwith reporting results monthly.\n\n\n               vi. Toxics\n\n   1) Reasonable Potential – The State of New Mexico\n\nThe Clean Water Act in Section 301 (b) requires that effluent limitations for point sources\ninclude any limitations necessary to meet water quality standards. Federal regulations found at\n40 CFR §122.44 (d) state that if a discharge poses the reasonable potential to cause an in-stream\nexcursion above a water quality criterion, the permit must contain an effluent limit for that\npollutant. All applicable facilities are required to fill out appropriate sections of the Form 2A\nand 2S, to apply for an NPDES permit or reissuance of an NPDES permit. The new form is\napplicable not only to POTWs, but also to facilities that are similar to POTWs, but which do not\nmeet the regulatory definition of “publicly owned treatment works” (like private domestics, or\nsimilar facilities on Federal property). The forms were designed and promulgated to “make it\neasier for permit applicants to provide the necessary information with their applications and\nminimize the need for additional follow-up requests from permitting authorities,” per the\nsummary statement in the preamble to the Rule. These forms became effective December 1,\n1999, after publication of the final rule on August 4, 1999, Volume 64, Number 149, pages\n42433 through 42527 of the FRL.\n\nThe facility is designated a major POTW for permitting purposes and must supply the expanded\npollutant testing list described in the EPA Application Form 2A. The City of Farmington WWTP\nis classified as a “major” discharger with a design flow more than 1.0 MGD. They completed\nPart D, “Expanded Effluent Testing Data” of form 2A and submitted to EPA as a part of their\nApril 13, 2021 application. The facility also submitted additional data on May 24, 2021. The\nsubmitted data are presented in the Table 1 of Section III.\nThe EPA conducted a water quality screen which is based on the March 15, 2012 NMIP to\ndetermine if discharged pollutant concentrations demonstrate RP to exceed WQS for the various\ndesignated uses. If RP exists, the screen model will calculate the appropriate permit limit needed\nto be protective of such designated uses.\n\nThe result of the preliminary RP analysis (see Appendix 2) indicates that Cyanide has RP to\nviolate WQS consistent with the designated uses for the receiving waterbody. The draft permit\nwill propose a daily maximum and monthly average limits of 22 ug/L and 14.67 ug/L,\n\fPERMIT NO. NM0020583                        FACT SHEET                              Page 14\nrespectively, for it. The facility shall have a 1-year compliance schedule to achieve final\nlimitations for Cyanide pollutant. The permit will require compliance schedule reports.\n\nThe preliminary toxic analysis, also, shows potential for RP to exist, based on insufficiently\nsensitive test methods used, for Cadmium, 2,3,7,8-TCDD Dioxin, Pentachlorophenol, Aldrin,\nChlordane and Toxaphene pollutants. Because the permittee has not met the sufficient sensitive\ntest requirement per 40 CFR 122.21(e)(3), EPA proposes monitoring for these parameters\nquarterly n the draft permit. During the public comment period, the permittee may submit the\nanalysis results using EPA approved methods with SSM complied MDL (shown in parenthesis)\nfor Cadmium (0.05 ug/L, EPA 200.9) , 2,3,7,8-TCDD Dioxin (1.0E-5 ug/L, EPA 1613B),\nPentachlorophenol (7.4 ug/L, EPA 604), Aldrin (0.004 ug/L, EPA 608), Chlordane (0.014 ug/L,\nEPA 608), and Toxaphene (0.24 ug/L, EPA 608)). EPA would reconsider this monitoring\nrequirement for the final permit if the result(s) indicate no reasonable potential exists.\n\n   2) Reasonable Potential – The Navajo Nation\n\nThe Navajo Nation is a downstream state and the permit limits developed for this permit must\nensure that its WQS are protected. The results of Navajo Nation RP screening (see Appendix 3)\nshow Cyanide pollutant demonstrates RP to exceed the Navajo Nation water quality standards\nconsistent with the designated uses for the receiving water. Monitoring Frequency for Limited Parameters\n\nRegulations require permits to establish monitoring requirements to yield data representative of\nthe monitored activity (40 CFR §122.48(b)) and to assure compliance with permit limitations (40\nCFR §122.44(i)(1)). Technology based pollutants; BOD5 and TSS, are proposed to be monitored\nfive times per week, with sampling on at least five different days. Flow is proposed to be\nmonitored continuously using a totalizing meter. Sample type for BOD5 and TSS is 12-Hr\ncomposite. These frequencies and sample types are the same as the current permit. The\nmonitoring frequencies of daily for TRC and five times per week for E. coli, pH and flow are\nconsistent with the previous permit. Whole Effluent Toxicity Requirements\n\nProcedures for implementing WET terms and conditions in NPDES permits are contained in the\nNMIP. The facility shall conduct a 48-hour acute test using Daphnia pulex once per\nquarter, and a 48-hour acute test using Pimephales promelas once per quarter. After the first four\nquarters without failures, the permittee may apply for a frequency reduction.
